  // Restore the saved color scheme preference, or
  // enact the browser preference if "automatic", 
  // without expecting DOM load to have completed.

  // Also restore any saved readingmode and ToC display preferences.
  function initializeReadingPreferences() {
    let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
    if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
      if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
        saved_theme = "dark";
      }
    }
    if (saved_theme == "dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
    }

    const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
    if (tocDisplay) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
    }
    const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
    if (readingMode) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
    }
  }
  // Run as soon as JS starts, to minimize repainting
  initializeReadingPreferences();

arXiv:2609.29531v2 [math.CA] 14 Oct 2026




The ultraspherical background of the Jacobi phase expansion, in closed form



Iván Area

I. AreaIFCAE, Universidade de Vigo, Departamento de Matemática Aplicada II, E. E. Aeronáutica e do Espazo, Campus As Lagoas s/n, 32004 Ourense, Spain

Email address, I. Area : area@uvigo.gal



Abstract.
    

The large-degree phase of the Jacobi polynomial
 MATH P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(\cos\theta) Pn(α,β)​(cos⁡θ)P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(\cos\theta) contains, at each order in
 MATH \mathcal{N}^{-1} 𝒩−1\mathcal{N}^{-1},  MATH \mathcal{N}=n+(\alpha+\beta+1)/2 𝒩=n+(α+β+1)/2\mathcal{N}=n+(\alpha+\beta+1)/2, an additive constant  MATH \kappa_{r} κr\kappa_{r}
that the phase equation does not determine. These constants are antisymmetric in
 MATH (\alpha^{2},\beta^{2}) (α2,β2)(\alpha^{2},\beta^{2}) and vanish for  MATH \alpha=\beta α=β\alpha=\beta. We obtain three
results. First, on the diagonal  MATH \alpha=\beta α=β\alpha=\beta the logarithm of the
midpoint amplitude is computed in closed form: if  MATH L_{n} LnL_{n} denotes the
coefficient of  MATH (2\mathcal{N})^{-n} (2​𝒩)−n(2\mathcal{N})^{-n} in that logarithm, then
 MATH \Upsilon_{A}(t)=\sum_{n\geq 1}L_{n}t^{n}/(n-1)! ΥA​(t)=∑n≥1Ln​tn/(n−1)!\Upsilon_{A}(t)=\sum_{n\geq 1}L_{n}t^{n}/(n-1)! equals
    




 MATH \Upsilon_{A}(t)=\frac{e^{t}\bigl(\cosh 2\alpha t-\cosh t\bigr)}{\sinh 2t}. ΥA​(t)=et​(cosh⁡2​α​t−cosh⁡t)sinh⁡2​t.\Upsilon_{A}(t)=\frac{e^{t}\bigl(\cosh 2\alpha t-\cosh t\bigr)}{\sinh 2t}.


    

The proof is algebraic: the identity is established for half-odd-integer
 MATH \alpha α\alpha, where the expansion terminates, by means of two classical
recurrences for Bessel polynomials, and is extended to all  MATH \alpha α\alpha by
a degree argument. Second, the derivative of the logarithm of the
amplitude with respect to  MATH \alpha^{2} α2\alpha^{2} at the Legendre point
 MATH \alpha=\beta=0 α=β=0\alpha=\beta=0 is computed in closed form to all orders. An exact reduction of an Appell
 MATH F_{3} F3F_{3} function to a Gauss function shows that it is a parameter
derivative of a zero-balanced  MATH {}_{2}F_{1} F12{}_{2}F_{1} at argument  MATH 2 22; the real part
of its expansion is a trigamma series, and the imaginary part is the
unique formal solution of an explicit central-difference equation. Third, we
deduce that the coefficients  MATH w_{m} wmw_{m} of an explicit even power series
attached to the imaginary part satisfy
 MATH \nu_{2}(w_{m})=-2\nu_{2}((2m)!) ν2​(wm)=−2​ν2​((2​m)!)\nu_{2}(w_{m})=-2\nu_{2}((2m)!) for every  MATH m\geq 0 m≥0m\geq 0 ( MATH \nu_{2} ν2\nu_{2} the  MATH 2 22-adic
valuation). This determines, for
every  MATH r rr, the exact power of  MATH 2 22 in the denominator of the coefficient
of  MATH \alpha^{2} α2\alpha^{2} in the polynomial that represents  MATH \kappa_{r} κr\kappa_{r}. We also give a factorisation of the
amplitude into products of pairs of Gauss functions, further valuation
formulas, and a numerical illustration for the zeros.
  



Key words and phrases: Jacobi polynomials; ultraspherical polynomials; Appell functions; Bessel polynomials; Borel transform; 2-adic valuation


2020 Mathematics Subject ClassificationPrimary 33C45; Secondary 33C05, 33C65, 41A60, 11S80




1. Introduction




We use the standard normalisation and notation for Jacobi polynomials
in [1, 2]; general background on hypergeometric and
classical special functions may be found in [3].
For  MATH \alpha,\beta>-1 α,β>−1\alpha,\beta>-1 the Jacobi polynomial  MATH P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)} Pn(α,β)P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)} is
orthogonal on  MATH [-1,1] [−1,1][-1,1] with respect to the weight
 MATH (1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta} (1−x)α​(1+x)β(1-x)^{\alpha}(1+x)^{\beta}, and its  MATH n nn zeros are real, simple and lie
in  MATH (-1,1) (−1,1)(-1,1); they are the nodes of Gauss–Jacobi quadrature. The
ultraspherical case  MATH \alpha=\beta α=β\alpha=\beta contains the Legendre polynomials
( MATH \alpha=\beta=0 α=β=0\alpha=\beta=0), the Chebyshev polynomials
( MATH \alpha=\beta=\pm\frac{1}{2} α=β=±12\alpha=\beta=\pm\frac{1}{2}) and the Gegenbauer polynomials. Whenever
the zeros of  MATH P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)} Pn(α,β)P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)} are considered as an ordered set in
 MATH (-1,1) (−1,1)(-1,1) (here and in Section 11) we assume
 MATH \alpha,\beta>-1 α,β>−1\alpha,\beta>-1. The formal and hypergeometric identities proved
below are polynomial or meromorphic in the parameters and, where
explicitly stated, hold beyond this range. Write the zeros as
 MATH x_{n,k}=\cos\theta_{n,k} xn,k=cos⁡θn,kx_{n,k}=\cos\theta_{n,k},  MATH 0<\theta_{n,1}<\dots<\theta_{n,n}<\pi 0<θn,1<⋯<θn,n<π0<\theta_{n,1}<\dots<\theta_{n,n}<\pi,
and put




 MATH \mathcal{N}=n+\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2},\qquad A=\alpha^{2},\qquad B=\beta^{2}. 𝒩=n+α+β+12,A=α2,B=β2.\mathcal{N}=n+\frac{\alpha+\beta+1}{2},\qquad A=\alpha^{2},\qquad B=\beta^{2}.






The starting point is the elementary observation that the zeros are
almost equally spaced in the angle  MATH \theta θ\theta. For the Chebyshev
polynomials this is exact: the zeros of  MATH T_{n} TnT_{n} are
 MATH \cos\bigl((k-\frac{1}{2})\pi/n\bigr) cos⁡((k−12)​π/n)\cos\bigl((k-\frac{1}{2})\pi/n\bigr). In general one has, for the zeros
away from the endpoints,



(1)

 MATH \theta_{n,k}=\frac{(k-\frac{1}{2})\pi+(\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{1}{4})\pi}{\mathcal{N}}+O(\mathcal{N}^{-2}), θn,k=(k−12)​π+(α2+14)​π𝒩+O⁡(𝒩−2),\theta_{n,k}=\frac{(k-\frac{1}{2})\pi+(\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{1}{4})\pi}{\mathcal{N}}+O(\mathcal{N}^{-2}),



and the question addressed by the phase expansion is what the
corrections look like, to all orders in  MATH \mathcal{N}^{-1} 𝒩−1\mathcal{N}^{-1}. The source
of (1) is the Liouville normal form of the Jacobi
differential equation: the function
 MATH u(\theta)=\sin^{\alpha+\frac{1}{2}}\!\frac{\theta}{2}\,\cos^{\beta+\frac{1}{2}}\!\frac{\theta}{2}\,P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(\cos\theta) u⁡(θ)=sinα+12⁡θ2​cosβ+12⁡θ2​Pn(α,β)​(cos⁡θ)u(\theta)=\sin^{\alpha+\frac{1}{2}}\!\frac{\theta}{2}\,\cos^{\beta+\frac{1}{2}}\!\frac{\theta}{2}\,P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(\cos\theta)
satisfies



(2)

 MATH u^{\prime\prime}(\theta)+\Bigl[\mathcal{N}^{2}+\frac{\frac{1}{4}-A}{4\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}}+\frac{\frac{1}{4}-B}{4\cos^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}}\Bigr]u(\theta)=0 u′′​(θ)+[𝒩2+14−A4​sin2⁡θ2+14−B4​cos2⁡θ2]​u​(θ)=0u^{\prime\prime}(\theta)+\Bigl[\mathcal{N}^{2}+\frac{\frac{1}{4}-A}{4\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}}+\frac{\frac{1}{4}-B}{4\cos^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}}\Bigr]u(\theta)=0



[1, Eq. (4.24.2)]. For  MATH A=B=\frac{1}{4} A=B=14A=B=\frac{1}{4} this is the equation of
a pure sinusoid of frequency  MATH \mathcal{N} 𝒩\mathcal{N}; in general  MATH u uu is a sinusoid of
frequency  MATH \mathcal{N} 𝒩\mathcal{N} whose phase is slowly deformed by the potential, and
the zeros of  MATH P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)} Pn(α,β)P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)} are the points where that phase
crosses the values  MATH (k-\frac{1}{2})\pi (k−12)​π(k-\frac{1}{2})\pi. Note that the
equation (2) depends on the parameters only through
 MATH A AA and  MATH B BB; this is why all the coefficients considered in this paper
are polynomials in  MATH A AA and  MATH B BB.




Solving the phase equation associated with (2)
recursively in powers of  MATH \mathcal{N}^{-2} 𝒩−2\mathcal{N}^{-2}, one obtains a phase function



(3)

 MATH \Xi(\theta)=\mathcal{N}\theta-\Bigl(\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{1}{4}\Bigr)\pi+\sum_{r\geq 1}\frac{\Psi_{r}(\theta)}{\mathcal{N}^{2r-1}}, Ξ⁡(θ)=𝒩​θ−(α2+14)​π+∑r≥1Ψr​(θ)𝒩2​r−1,\Xi(\theta)=\mathcal{N}\theta-\Bigl(\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{1}{4}\Bigr)\pi+\sum_{r\geq 1}\frac{\Psi_{r}(\theta)}{\mathcal{N}^{2r-1}},



such that the zeros are, to all orders in  MATH 1/\mathcal{N} 1/𝒩1/\mathcal{N}, the solutions of
 MATH \Xi(\theta_{n,k})=(k-\frac{1}{2})\pi Ξ⁡(θn,k)=(k−12)​π\Xi(\theta_{n,k})=(k-\frac{1}{2})\pi. The first term is elementary,




 MATH \Psi_{1}(\theta)=\frac{(1-4B)\tan\frac{\theta}{2}-(1-4A)\cot\frac{\theta}{2}}{16}, Ψ1​(θ)=(1−4​B)​tan⁡θ2−(1−4​A)​cot⁡θ216,\Psi_{1}(\theta)=\frac{(1-4B)\tan\frac{\theta}{2}-(1-4A)\cot\frac{\theta}{2}}{16},



and already very effective: for  MATH n=20 n=20n=20,  MATH \alpha=\frac{3}{2} α=32\alpha=\frac{3}{2},
 MATH \beta=\frac{1}{4} β=14\beta=\frac{1}{4}, the maximal error of (1) on the ten
central zeros is  MATH 2.2\cdot 10^{-3} 2.2⋅10−32.2\cdot 10^{-3}, and adding the term
 MATH \Psi_{1}(\theta)/\mathcal{N} Ψ1​(θ)/𝒩\Psi_{1}(\theta)/\mathcal{N} reduces it to  MATH 3.9\cdot 10^{-6} 3.9⋅10−63.9\cdot 10^{-6}. Each  MATH \Psi_{r} Ψr\Psi_{r} is
obtained from the recursion by an integration and is therefore
determined only up to an additive constant. We normalise  MATH \Psi_{r} Ψr\Psi_{r} to
be an odd Laurent polynomial in  MATH \cot(\theta/2) cot⁡(θ/2)\cot(\theta/2), without constant
term; the information not fixed by the differential equation is then
carried by the value at the midpoint,




 MATH \kappa_{r}=\Psi_{r}(\pi/2). κr=Ψr​(π/2).\kappa_{r}=\Psi_{r}(\pi/2).



The numbers  MATH \kappa_{r} κr\kappa_{r}, which are polynomials in  MATH A AA and  MATH B BB with
rational coefficients, are the only part of (3)
that the phase equation does not produce. We call them the
phase constants. The first three are



(4)

 MATH \begin{gathered}\kappa_{1}=\frac{A-B}{4},\qquad\kappa_{2}=\frac{(A-B)(2A+2B-7)}{96},\\
\kappa_{3}=\frac{(A-B)(28A^{2}+88AB+28B^{2}-380A-380B+781)}{7680}.\end{gathered} κ1=A−B4,κ2=(A−B)​(2​A+2​B−7)96,κ3=(A−B)​(28​A2+88​A​B+28​B2−380​A−380​B+781)7680.\begin{gathered}\kappa_{1}=\frac{A-B}{4},\qquad\kappa_{2}=\frac{(A-B)(2A+2B-7)}{96},\\
\kappa_{3}=\frac{(A-B)(28A^{2}+88AB+28B^{2}-380A-380B+781)}{7680}.\end{gathered}






Two features of (4) persist for all  MATH r rr and motivate the
paper. The first is antisymmetry. Since
 MATH P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(-x)=(-1)^{n}P_{n}^{(\beta,\alpha)}(x) Pn(α,β)​(−x)=(−1)n​Pn(β,α)​(x)P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}(-x)=(-1)^{n}P_{n}^{(\beta,\alpha)}(x), reflection at
 MATH \theta=\pi/2 θ=π/2\theta=\pi/2 interchanges  MATH \alpha α\alpha and  MATH \beta β\beta and reverses the sign
of the phase, so



(5)

 MATH \kappa_{r}(B,A)=-\kappa_{r}(A,B),\qquad\text{hence}\qquad(A-B)\mid\kappa_{r}. κr​(B,A)=−κr​(A,B),hence(A−B)|κr.\kappa_{r}(B,A)=-\kappa_{r}(A,B),\qquad\text{hence}\qquad(A-B)\mid\kappa_{r}.



In particular  MATH \kappa_{r} κr\kappa_{r} vanishes identically on the diagonal  MATH A=B A=BA=B,
which contains the ultraspherical case  MATH \alpha=\beta α=β\alpha=\beta: the Legendre and
Gegenbauer cases need no phase constants. The nontrivial content
of (3) is therefore transverse to the diagonal. On
the diagonal itself the phase constants vanish but the series from
which they are extracted (the amplitude, introduced below) does not;
it is a nontrivial asymptotic series. Our first question is whether
this series can be computed in closed form. The second feature is
arithmetic: the denominators  MATH 4=2^{2} 4=224=2^{2},  MATH 96=2^{5}\cdot 3 96=25⋅396=2^{5}\cdot 3,
 MATH 7680=2^{9}\cdot 15 7680=29⋅157680=2^{9}\cdot 15 and, for  MATH \kappa_{4} κ4\kappa_{4},  MATH 86016=2^{12}\cdot 21 86016=212⋅2186016=2^{12}\cdot 21 grow
mainly through powers of  MATH 2 22. In [17] it is
showed that the power of  MATH 2 22 in the denominator of  MATH \kappa_{r} κr\kappa_{r} is at
most  MATH 2^{E_{r}} 2Er2^{E_{r}} with  MATH E_{r}=3r-1+\nu_{2}((r-1)!) Er=3​r−1+ν2​((r−1)!)E_{r}=3r-1+\nu_{2}((r-1)!), where  MATH \nu_{2} ν2\nu_{2} denotes
the  MATH 2 22-adic valuation. Our second question is whether this bound is
attained for every  MATH r rr. Both questions are answered in this paper:
the diagonal series is the Stirling series of a quotient of Gamma
functions (Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2), and the bound  MATH 2^{E_{r}} 2Er2^{E_{r}} is sharp for every
 MATH r rr (Theorem 1.5). The proof of the second statement
uses the first: the closed form on the diagonal is the background over
which the first-order variation transverse to the diagonal is
computed.




Large-degree asymptotics for Jacobi polynomials and functions have a
long history, from the classical trigonometric and uniform expansions
of Hahn [9] and Frenzen–Wong [10] to
Riemann–Hilbert treatments of Jacobi-type weights
[12], fast computation of Gauss–Jacobi
nodes and weights [11, 14],
nonoscillatory phase-function methods [13], explicit
error bounds [16] and, closest to our starting
point, the inverse-factorial large-degree expansions of
Nemes [15] for Jacobi and related functions with fixed
parameters, which carry explicit computable error bounds, are built
from the same Hankel coefficients as (6) below, and terminate
at half-odd-integer parameters. These works develop the expansions,
their error bounds and the associated algorithms. The present paper
addresses a different question: the exact algebraic and arithmetic
structure of the coefficients of the phase expansion, and in particular
of the constants  MATH \kappa_{r} κr\kappa_{r}.




We now state the main results, which are proved in the following
sections. Let




 MATH a_{k}(\nu)=\frac{(\frac{1}{2}+\nu)_{k}(\frac{1}{2}-\nu)_{k}}{(-2)^{k}k!}=\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\bigl(4\nu^{2}-(2j+1)^{2}\bigr)}{8^{k}k!} ak​(ν)=(12+ν)k​(12−ν)k(−2)k​k!=∏j=0k−1(4​ν2−(2​j+1)2)8k​k!a_{k}(\nu)=\frac{(\frac{1}{2}+\nu)_{k}(\frac{1}{2}-\nu)_{k}}{(-2)^{k}k!}=\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\bigl(4\nu^{2}-(2j+1)^{2}\bigr)}{8^{k}k!}



be the Hankel coefficients, and put  MATH Z=2\mathcal{N} Z=2​𝒩Z=2\mathcal{N}. The large-degree
behaviour of the Jacobi polynomials at the midpoint  MATH \theta=\pi/2 θ=π/2\theta=\pi/2 is
carried by the inverse-factorial series



(6)

 MATH \begin{gathered}F(Z)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{G_{n}}{(Z-1)(Z-2)\cdots(Z-n)},\\
G_{n}=\sum_{l=0}^{n}a_{l}(\alpha)a_{n-l}(\beta)\,p^{\,l}q^{\,n-l},\qquad p=1+i,\quad q=1-i,\end{gathered} F⁡(Z)=∑n≥0Gn(Z−1)(Z−2)⋯(Z−n),Gn=∑l=0nal(α)an−l(β)plqn−l,p=1+i,q=1−i,\begin{gathered}F(Z)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{G_{n}}{(Z-1)(Z-2)\cdots(Z-n)},\\
G_{n}=\sum_{l=0}^{n}a_{l}(\alpha)a_{n-l}(\beta)\,p^{\,l}q^{\,n-l},\qquad p=1+i,\quad q=1-i,\end{gathered}



which we call the amplitude. Write
 MATH \log F=\sum_{n\geq 1}L_{n}Z^{-n} log⁡F=∑n≥1Ln​Z−n\log F=\sum_{n\geq 1}L_{n}Z^{-n}; the coefficients  MATH L_{n} LnL_{n} are polynomials
in  MATH A AA and  MATH B BB. The phase constants are the imaginary parts of the odd
coefficients,



(7)

 MATH \kappa_{r}=2^{-(2r-1)}\operatorname{Im}L_{2r-1}. κr=2−(2​r−1)​Im⁡L2​r−1.\kappa_{r}=2^{-(2r-1)}\operatorname{Im}L_{2r-1}.



Formula (7) is the definition of  MATH \kappa_{r} κr\kappa_{r} used in
this paper; its agreement with the constants
 MATH \Psi_{r}(\pi/2) Ψr​(π/2)\Psi_{r}(\pi/2) of (3) is discussed in
Proposition 7.37.




Our first result concerns the diagonal  MATH A=B=\alpha^{2} A=B=α2A=B=\alpha^{2}. Let
 MATH L_{n}^{\mathrm{diag}} LndiagL_{n}^{\mathrm{diag}} be the value of  MATH L_{n} LnL_{n} there, and let




 MATH \Upsilon_{A}(t)=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{L_{n}^{\mathrm{diag}}}{(n-1)!}\,t^{n} ΥA​(t)=∑n≥1Lndiag(n−1)!​tn\Upsilon_{A}(t)=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{L_{n}^{\mathrm{diag}}}{(n-1)!}\,t^{n}



be the associated generating function. We write  MATH \mathcal{B}_{k}(x) ℬk​(x)\mathcal{B}_{k}(x) for the Bernoulli
polynomials and  MATH \mathcal{B}_{k}=\mathcal{B}_{k}(0) ℬk=ℬk​(0)\mathcal{B}_{k}=\mathcal{B}_{k}(0) for the Bernoulli numbers, so that
 MATH \mathcal{B}_{1}=-\frac{1}{2} ℬ1=−12\mathcal{B}_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}, and  MATH \mathsf{E}_{2k} 𝖤2​k\mathsf{E}_{2k} for the Euler numbers. As usual,
 MATH (a)_{k}=a(a+1)\cdots(a+k-1) (a)k=a(a+1)⋯(a+k−1)(a)_{k}=a(a+1)\cdots(a+k-1) is the Pochhammer symbol,
 MATH \psi=\Gamma^{\prime}/\Gamma ψ=Γ′/Γ\psi=\Gamma^{\prime}/\Gamma is the digamma function,  MATH \operatorname{Li}_{k} Lik\operatorname{Li}_{k} is
the polylogarithm, and  MATH [x^{k}]f [xk]​f[x^{k}]f denotes the coefficient of  MATH x^{k} xkx^{k} in a
power series  MATH f ff.



Theorem 1.1.



For every  MATH \alpha α\alpha,

Theorem 1.6.

where  MATH \widehat{q}_{n} q^n\widehat{q}_{n} is the coefficient of  MATH Z^{-n} Z−nZ^{-n} in

The proof of Theorem 1.6 proceeds as follows. The
amplitude (6) is a Kampé de Fériet double series
(Lemma 7.2), in fact an Appell  MATH F_{3} F3F_{3} function evaluated on a
curve on which it reduces to a single Gauss function
(Theorem 7.3). Consequently
 MATH \sum_{n}g_{n}Z^{-n} ∑ngn​Z−n\sum_{n}g_{n}Z^{-n} is the expansion of a second parameter derivative of
a zero-balanced  MATH {}_{2}F_{1} F12{}_{2}F_{1} at the boundary value  MATH 2+i0 2+i​02+i0
(Corollary 7.4). The classical connection formula
splits this boundary value into two blocks which are quotients of Gamma
functions at the Legendre point (Proposition 7.7); the
variation of one of them satisfies a first-order difference equation
which is solved by quadratures (Theorem 7.9); and the
passage from the resulting convergent expressions to the formal series
in  MATH Z^{-1} Z−1Z^{-1} is made by an asymptotic matching along a vertical line
(Subsection 7.6), whose estimates are given in
Appendix A. Theorem 1.5 then follows from
the  MATH 2 22-adic analysis of the explicit series  MATH \widehat{Q}_{\Gamma} Q^Γ\widehat{Q}_{\Gamma}
(Subsection 7.7): the exponent of the Gamma quotient
has Genocchi-number coefficients, and in the expansion of its
exponential exactly one term has minimal valuation.




Besides these results the paper contains further exact structure of
the amplitude. The recurrences for Bessel polynomials used in the
proof of Theorem 1.1 do not require the special relation
 MATH p^{2}+q^{2}=0 p2+q2=0p^{2}+q^{2}=0 satisfied at the midpoint (Theorem 4.1); on
the whole curve described by the weights  MATH p,q p,qp,q as  MATH \theta θ\theta varies,
where  MATH p+q=pq p+q=p​qp+q=pq, the half-odd-integer amplitudes satisfy a three-term
contiguity whose numerators are given in closed form in terms of ballot
numbers (Theorems 5.2 and 5.4), and the
derivatives of all orders normal to that curve are determined
recursively (Theorem 6.4). For independent
half-odd-integer parameters  MATH \alpha,\beta α,β\alpha,\beta the amplitudes satisfy a
similar recurrence off the diagonal (Theorem 7.1). The
amplitude is an explicit sum of products of pairs of Gauss functions,
the  MATH r rr-th term being of order  MATH Z^{-3r} Z−3​rZ^{-3r}
(Corollary 8.2); from this expansion we derive four
valuation formulas for the numerator and the denominator of
 MATH \sum_{n}g_{n}Z^{-n} ∑ngn​Z−n\sum_{n}g_{n}Z^{-n} (Theorem 10.3 and
Proposition 10.4). Section 11 illustrates the
use of (3) for the numerical computation of zeros.




Two statements of the paper are not proved in full generality, and
neither is used in the proofs of Theorems 1.1,
1.5 and 1.6. The first is the agreement
of (7) with the constants  MATH \Psi_{r}(\pi/2) Ψr​(π/2)\Psi_{r}(\pi/2)
of (3), which is proved for  MATH r\leq 8 r≤8r\leq 8
(Proposition 7.37); for  MATH r>8 r>8r>8 the last assertion of
Theorem 1.5 is a statement about the polynomials defined
by (7). The second is a periodicity hypothesis under
which the function whose expansion is  MATH \sum_{n}g_{n}Z^{-n} ∑ngn​Z−n\sum_{n}g_{n}Z^{-n} is evaluated in
closed form on the real axis (Proposition 7.28).




Throughout the paper, identities in  MATH Z^{-1} Z−1Z^{-1} (equivalently in
 MATH v=Z^{-1} v=Z−1v=Z^{-1} or in  MATH s^{-1} s−1s^{-1},  MATH s=(1-Z)/2 s=(1−Z)/2s=(1-Z)/2) are identities of formal
Laurent series unless convergence is explicitly asserted. When a
meromorphic function is introduced, its expansion for large  MATH Z ZZ is
always understood in the stated sector. For half-odd-integer
parameters the identities are identities of rational functions or of
quotients of Gamma functions.




The paper is organised as follows. Sections 2
and 3 prove Theorem 1.1 and its
corollaries. Sections 4, 5
and 6 treat the half-odd-integer amplitudes away
from the midpoint. Section 7 contains the proofs of
Theorems 1.6 and 1.5: the Appell reduction
(Subsection 7.1), the boundary split and the quadratures
(Subsections 7.2 and 7.3), the asymptotic
matching (Subsection 7.6) and the arithmetic
(Subsection 7.7); Subsections 7.4
and 7.5 evaluate the boundary value exactly on the real
axis and are used only in Proposition 7.28. The proofs
of Theorems 1.6 and 1.5 are contained in
Section 7 and Appendix A; apart from
the notation of the Introduction and Lemma 3.1, they do
not depend on Sections 4–6
or 8–11.
Section 8 gives the expansion of the amplitude into
products of Gauss functions, Section 9 the linear
system satisfied by the transverse variation, and
Section 10 the valuation formulas.
Section 11 contains the numerical illustration, and
Appendix A the estimates used in
Subsection 7.6.

for  MATH k\leq\ell k≤ℓk\leq\ell, and it vanishes for  MATH k>\ell k>ℓk>\ell. Dividing by  MATH 8^{k}k! 8k​k!8^{k}k! gives
the stated value.
∎

2.2. Two classical recurrences, and their consequence



Lemma 2.2.



For  MATH n\geq 0 n≥0n\geq 0, with the convention  MATH y_{-1}(x)=1 y−1​(x)=1y_{-1}(x)=1,




 MATH \text{(a)}\quad x^{2}y_{n}^{\prime}(x)=(nx-1)y_{n}(x)+y_{n-1}(x),\qquad\text{(b)}\quad y_{n+1}(x)=(2n+1)x\,y_{n}(x)+y_{n-1}(x). (a)x2​yn′​(x)=(n​x−1)​yn​(x)+yn−1​(x),(b)yn+1​(x)=(2​n+1)​x​yn​(x)+yn−1​(x).\text{(a)}\quad x^{2}y_{n}^{\prime}(x)=(nx-1)y_{n}(x)+y_{n-1}(x),\qquad\text{(b)}\quad y_{n+1}(x)=(2n+1)x\,y_{n}(x)+y_{n-1}(x).






Proof.



Both identities follow by comparing coefficients in the defining sum;
they are classical [4, 5, 6].
∎




Definition 2.3.



 MATH \displaystyle C_{\ell}(\xi)=\tfrac{1}{2}\Bigl(q\,y_{\ell-1}(p\xi)y_{\ell}(q\xi)+p\,y_{\ell}(p\xi)y_{\ell-1}(q\xi)\Bigr) Cℓ​(ξ)=12​(q​yℓ−1​(p​ξ)​yℓ​(q​ξ)+p​yℓ​(p​ξ)​yℓ−1​(q​ξ))\displaystyle C_{\ell}(\xi)=\tfrac{1}{2}\Bigl(q\,y_{\ell-1}(p\xi)y_{\ell}(q\xi)+p\,y_{\ell}(p\xi)y_{\ell-1}(q\xi)\Bigr),
which is real because the two terms are complex conjugate.




Proposition 2.4.



 MATH \xi^{2}\Pi_{\ell}^{\prime}(\xi)+\bigl(1-2\ell\xi\bigr)\Pi_{\ell}(\xi)=C_{\ell}(\xi) ξ2​Πℓ′​(ξ)+(1−2​ℓ​ξ)​Πℓ​(ξ)=Cℓ​(ξ)\xi^{2}\Pi_{\ell}^{\prime}(\xi)+\bigl(1-2\ell\xi\bigr)\Pi_{\ell}(\xi)=C_{\ell}(\xi).




Proof.



Put  MATH U=y_{\ell}(p\xi) U=yℓ​(p​ξ)U=y_{\ell}(p\xi),  MATH V=y_{\ell}(q\xi) V=yℓ​(q​ξ)V=y_{\ell}(q\xi),  MATH U_{-}=y_{\ell-1}(p\xi) U−=yℓ−1​(p​ξ)U_{-}=y_{\ell-1}(p\xi),  MATH V_{-}=y_{\ell-1}(q\xi) V−=yℓ−1​(q​ξ)V_{-}=y_{\ell-1}(q\xi).  MATH \xi^{2}\Pi_{\ell}^{\prime}=\xi^{2}(U^{\prime}V+UV^{\prime})=\frac{(\ell p\xi-1)U+U_{-}}{p}\,V+U\,\frac{(\ell q\xi-1)V+V_{-}}{q}\\
=\Bigl(2\ell\xi-\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}\Bigr)UV+\frac{U_{-}V}{p}+\frac{UV_{-}}{q}. ξ2​Πℓ′=ξ2​(U′​V+U​V′)=(ℓ​p​ξ−1)​U+U−p​V+U​(ℓ​q​ξ−1)​V+V−q=(2​ℓ​ξ−1p−1q)​U​V+U−​Vp+U​V−q.\xi^{2}\Pi_{\ell}^{\prime}=\xi^{2}(U^{\prime}V+UV^{\prime})=\frac{(\ell p\xi-1)U+U_{-}}{p}\,V+U\,\frac{(\ell q\xi-1)V+V_{-}}{q}\\
=\Bigl(2\ell\xi-\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}\Bigr)UV+\frac{U_{-}V}{p}+\frac{UV_{-}}{q}.



By (15),  MATH \frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1 1p+1q=1\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1,  MATH \frac{1}{p}=\frac{q}{2} 1p=q2\frac{1}{p}=\frac{q}{2} and  MATH \frac{1}{q}=\frac{p}{2} 1q=p2\frac{1}{q}=\frac{p}{2}, so the right-hand side is  MATH (2\ell\xi-1)\Pi_{\ell}+C_{\ell} (2​ℓ​ξ−1)​Πℓ+Cℓ(2\ell\xi-1)\Pi_{\ell}+C_{\ell}.
∎




Proposition 2.5.



 MATH C_{\ell+2}=C_{\ell}+(4\ell+2)\,\xi\,\Pi_{\ell} Cℓ+2=Cℓ+(4​ℓ+2)​ξ​ΠℓC_{\ell+2}=C_{\ell}+(4\ell+2)\,\xi\,\Pi_{\ell}.




Proof.

and the first term vanishes by (15). Applying

using  MATH pq=2 p​q=2pq=2. Dividing by  MATH 2 22 gives the claim.
∎

Theorem 2.10.



For every  MATH \ell\geq 0 ℓ≥0\ell\geq 0, with  MATH \zeta=Z/4 ζ=Z/4\zeta=Z/4,




 MATH F_{\ell}(Z)=\mathcal{M}_{\ell}(Z):=\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\zeta-\frac{\ell}{2}\bigr)\,\Gamma\bigl(\zeta+\frac{\ell+1}{2}\bigr)}{\Gamma(\zeta)\,\Gamma\bigl(\zeta+\frac{1}{2}\bigr)}. Proof.

 MATH \frac{\mathcal{M}_{\ell+2}}{\mathcal{M}_{\ell}}=\frac{\Gamma(\zeta-\frac{\ell}{2}-1)}{\Gamma(\zeta-\frac{\ell}{2})}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(\zeta+\frac{\ell+3}{2})}{\Gamma(\zeta+\frac{\ell+1}{2})}=\frac{\zeta+\frac{\ell+1}{2}}{\zeta-\frac{\ell+2}{2}}=\frac{Z+2\ell+2}{Z-2\ell-4}, ℳℓ+2ℳℓ=Γ⁡(ζ−ℓ2−1)Γ⁡(ζ−ℓ2)⋅Γ⁡(ζ+ℓ+32)Γ⁡(ζ+ℓ+12)=ζ+ℓ+12ζ−ℓ+22=Z+2​ℓ+2Z−2​ℓ−4,\frac{\mathcal{M}_{\ell+2}}{\mathcal{M}_{\ell}}=\frac{\Gamma(\zeta-\frac{\ell}{2}-1)}{\Gamma(\zeta-\frac{\ell}{2})}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(\zeta+\frac{\ell+3}{2})}{\Gamma(\zeta+\frac{\ell+1}{2})}=\frac{\zeta+\frac{\ell+1}{2}}{\zeta-\frac{\ell+2}{2}}=\frac{Z+2\ell+2}{Z-2\ell-4},



which is Proposition 2.8. For the base cases,  MATH \mathcal{M}_{0}=1 ℳ0=1\mathcal{M}_{0}=1 and




 MATH \mathcal{M}_{1}=\frac{\Gamma(\zeta-\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\zeta+\frac{1}{2})}\frac{\Gamma(\zeta+1)}{\Gamma(\zeta)}=\frac{\zeta}{\zeta-\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{Z}{Z-2}, ℳ1=Γ⁡(ζ−12)Γ⁡(ζ+12)​Γ⁡(ζ+1)Γ⁡(ζ)=ζζ−12=ZZ−2,\mathcal{M}_{1}=\frac{\Gamma(\zeta-\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\zeta+\frac{1}{2})}\frac{\Gamma(\zeta+1)}{\Gamma(\zeta)}=\frac{\zeta}{\zeta-\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{Z}{Z-2},

3. From half-integers to all  MATH \alpha α\alpha

Lemma 3.2.

 MATH \mathcal{M}(\zeta;\alpha)=\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\zeta+\frac{1-2\alpha}{4}\bigr)\Gamma\bigl(\zeta+\frac{1+2\alpha}{4}\bigr)}{\Gamma(\zeta)\,\Gamma\bigl(\zeta+\frac{1}{2}\bigr)}.  MATH \log\mathcal{M}(\zeta;\alpha)\sim\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{\chi_{n}(\alpha)}{n(n+1)}\,\zeta^{-n}, log⁡ℳ⁡(ζ,α)∼∑n≥1χn​(α)n⁡(n+1)​ζ−n,\log\mathcal{M}(\zeta;\alpha)\sim\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{\chi_{n}(\alpha)}{n(n+1)}\,\zeta^{-n},






 MATH \chi_{n}(\alpha)=\mathcal{B}_{n+1}\bigl(\tfrac{3+2\alpha}{4}\bigr)+\mathcal{B}_{n+1}\bigl(\tfrac{3-2\alpha}{4}\bigr)-\mathcal{B}_{n+1}(1)-\mathcal{B}_{n+1}\bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\bigr). χn​(α)=ℬn+1​(3+2​α4)+ℬn+1​(3−2​α4)−ℬn+1​(1)−ℬn+1​(12).\chi_{n}(\alpha)=\mathcal{B}_{n+1}\bigl(\tfrac{3+2\alpha}{4}\bigr)+\mathcal{B}_{n+1}\bigl(\tfrac{3-2\alpha}{4}\bigr)-\mathcal{B}_{n+1}(1)-\mathcal{B}_{n+1}\bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\bigr).



Moreover  MATH \mathcal{M}_{\ell} ℳℓ\mathcal{M}_{\ell} of Theorem 2.10 is the value of
the left-hand quotient at  MATH \alpha=\ell+\frac{1}{2} α=ℓ+12\alpha=\ell+\frac{1}{2}.




Proof.

 MATH \log\frac{\Gamma(\zeta+a)}{\Gamma(\zeta+b)}-(a-b)\log\zeta\sim\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{n(n+1)}\bigl(\mathcal{B}_{n+1}(a)-\mathcal{B}_{n+1}(b)\bigr)\zeta^{-n} log⁡Γ⁡(ζ+a)Γ⁡(ζ+b)−(a−b)​log⁡ζ∼∑n≥1(−1)n+1n⁡(n+1)​(ℬn+1​(a)−ℬn+1​(b))​ζ−n\log\frac{\Gamma(\zeta+a)}{\Gamma(\zeta+b)}-(a-b)\log\zeta\sim\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{n(n+1)}\bigl(\mathcal{B}_{n+1}(a)-\mathcal{B}_{n+1}(b)\bigr)\zeta^{-n}



applies with  MATH a=\frac{1\mp 2\alpha}{4} a=1∓2​α4a=\frac{1\mp 2\alpha}{4} and  MATH b\in\{0,\frac{1}{2}\} b∈{0,12}b\in\{0,\frac{1}{2}\}; the logarithmic

 MATH 1-\frac{1\mp 2\alpha}{4}=\frac{3\pm 2\alpha}{4} 1−1∓2​α4=3±2​α41-\frac{1\mp 2\alpha}{4}=\frac{3\pm 2\alpha}{4},  MATH 1-0=1 1−0=11-0=1,  MATH 1-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{2} 1−12=121-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{2}
gives the stated form. For the second assertion, at

 MATH \frac{1+2\alpha}{4}=\frac{\ell+1}{2} 1+2​α4=ℓ+12\frac{1+2\alpha}{4}=\frac{\ell+1}{2}.

Proof of Theorem 1.1.



Fix  MATH n nn. By Lemma 3.1 both sides of (9) are
polynomials in  MATH A AA of degree at most  MATH n nn. By Theorem 2.10,
 MATH F=\mathcal{M}_{\ell} F=ℳℓF=\mathcal{M}_{\ell} at  MATH \alpha=\ell+\frac{1}{2} α=ℓ+12\alpha=\ell+\frac{1}{2} for every  MATH \ell\geq 0 ℓ≥0\ell\geq 0; both sides
being rational functions of  MATH Z ZZ there, their expansions at  MATH Z=\infty Z=∞Z=\infty
coincide, and by Lemma 3.2 the coefficients of
 MATH \log\mathcal{M}_{\ell} log⁡ℳℓ\log\mathcal{M}_{\ell} are the right-hand side of (9) evaluated at
 MATH A=(\ell+\frac{1}{2})^{2} A=(ℓ+12)2A=(\ell+\frac{1}{2})^{2}. Hence the two polynomials agree at the infinitely many
distinct points  MATH A=(\ell+\frac{1}{2})^{2} A=(ℓ+12)2A=(\ell+\frac{1}{2})^{2},  MATH \ell=0,1,2,\dots ℓ=0,1,2,…\ell=0,1,2,\dots, and therefore
coincide. This proves (9).




For (8), sum (9) with the generating function
 MATH \sum_{k}\mathcal{B}_{k}(x)u^{k}/k!=ue^{xu}/(e^{u}-1) ∑kℬk​(x)​uk/k!=u​ex​u/(eu−1)\sum_{k}\mathcal{B}_{k}(x)u^{k}/k!=ue^{xu}/(e^{u}-1) at  MATH u=4t u=4​tu=4t. The constant and linear
corrections cancel because
 MATH \frac{3+2\alpha}{4}+\frac{3-2\alpha}{4}-1-\frac{1}{2}=0 3+2​α4+3−2​α4−1−12=0\frac{3+2\alpha}{4}+\frac{3-2\alpha}{4}-1-\frac{1}{2}=0, and one obtains




 MATH \Upsilon_{A}(t)=\frac{e^{(3+2\alpha)t}+e^{(3-2\alpha)t}-e^{4t}-e^{2t}}{e^{4t}-1}=\frac{2e^{3t}\bigl(\cosh 2\alpha t-\cosh t\bigr)}{2e^{2t}\sinh 2t}, ΥA​(t)=e(3+2​α)​t+e(3−2​α)​t−e4​t−e2​te4​t−1=2​e3​t​(cosh⁡2​α​t−cosh⁡t)2​e2​t​sinh⁡2​t,\Upsilon_{A}(t)=\frac{e^{(3+2\alpha)t}+e^{(3-2\alpha)t}-e^{4t}-e^{2t}}{e^{4t}-1}=\frac{2e^{3t}\bigl(\cosh 2\alpha t-\cosh t\bigr)}{2e^{2t}\sinh 2t},



which is (8).
∎




Proof of Corollary 1.2.



By Theorem 1.1 and Lemma 3.2, the formal
Laurent series  MATH \log F log⁡F\log F on the diagonal is the Stirling series of
 MATH \Gamma(\zeta+\frac{1-2\alpha}{4})\Gamma(\zeta+\frac{1+2\alpha}{4})/\bigl(\Gamma(\zeta)\Gamma(\zeta+\frac{1}{2})\bigr) Γ⁡(ζ+1−2​α4)​Γ​(ζ+1+2​α4)/(Γ⁡(ζ)​Γ​(ζ+12))\Gamma(\zeta+\frac{1-2\alpha}{4})\Gamma(\zeta+\frac{1+2\alpha}{4})/\bigl(\Gamma(\zeta)\Gamma(\zeta+\frac{1}{2})\bigr) with  MATH \zeta=Z/4 ζ=Z/4\zeta=Z/4, whose ratio
at  MATH \zeta\mapsto\zeta+1 ζ↦ζ+1\zeta\mapsto\zeta+1 is
 MATH \bigl(\zeta+\frac{1-2\alpha}{4}\bigr)\bigl(\zeta+\frac{1+2\alpha}{4}\bigr)/\bigl(\zeta(\zeta+\frac{1}{2})\bigr)=\bigl((Z+1)^{2}-4A\bigr)/\bigl(Z(Z+2)\bigr) (ζ+1−2​α4)​(ζ+1+2​α4)/(ζ⁡(ζ+12))=((Z+1)2−4​A)/(Z⁡(Z+2))\bigl(\zeta+\frac{1-2\alpha}{4}\bigr)\bigl(\zeta+\frac{1+2\alpha}{4}\bigr)/\bigl(\zeta(\zeta+\frac{1}{2})\bigr)=\bigl((Z+1)^{2}-4A\bigr)/\bigl(Z(Z+2)\bigr).
Thus the ratio identity is first obtained coefficientwise in  MATH Z^{-1} Z−1Z^{-1}.
Exponentiating is legitimate because both formal logarithms have zero
constant term, and gives the first displayed contiguity identity. Multiplying
by the exact quotient
 MATH \Gamma(Z+4)/\Gamma(Z)=Z(Z+1)(Z+2)(Z+3) Γ⁡(Z+4)/Γ⁡(Z)=Z⁡(Z+1)​(Z+2)​(Z+3)\Gamma(Z+4)/\Gamma(Z)=Z(Z+1)(Z+2)(Z+3) gives the second identity.
∎




Remark 3.3.



Conversely, the contiguity of Corollary 1.2 determines

5. The physical curve



Lemma 5.1.



For a general angle  MATH 0<\theta<\pi 0<θ<π0<\theta<\pi the series (6) is taken with
the weights  MATH p=2/(1-e^{i\theta}) p=2/(1−ei​θ)p=2/(1-e^{i\theta}),  MATH q=2/(1+e^{i\theta}) q=2/(1+ei​θ)q=2/(1+e^{i\theta}). They satisfy




 MATH p+q=pq=\frac{4}{1-e^{2i\theta}}=:\varpi,\qquad\text{hence}\qquad\sigma=1,\qquad\rho=\varpi-2 p+q=p​q=41−e2​i​θ=:ϖ,henceσ=1,ρ=ϖ−2p+q=pq=\frac{4}{1-e^{2i\theta}}=:\varpi,\qquad\text{hence}\qquad\sigma=1,\qquad\rho=\varpi-2



identically in  MATH \theta θ\theta, not only at the midpoint  MATH \theta=\pi/2 θ=π/2\theta=\pi/2 (where  MATH \varpi=2 ϖ=2\varpi=2).
Equivalently, in the variables  MATH x=p/2 x=p/2x=p/2,  MATH y=q/2 y=q/2y=q/2, the weights lie on the
curve  MATH x+y=2xy x+y=2​x​yx+y=2xy, which we call the physical curve;  MATH x xx and  MATH y yy are
the Horn variables.




Proof.



 MATH p+q=2\bigl[(1+e^{i\theta})+(1-e^{i\theta})\bigr]/(1-e^{2i\theta})=4/(1-e^{2i\theta})=pq p+q=2​[(1+ei​θ)+(1−ei​θ)]/(1−e2​i​θ)=4/(1−e2​i​θ)=p​qp+q=2\bigl[(1+e^{i\theta})+(1-e^{i\theta})\bigr]/(1-e^{2i\theta})=4/(1-e^{2i\theta})=pq.
∎

Remark 5.5.



The curve  MATH \sigma=1 σ=1\sigma=1 passes through the midpoint in the plane of the
Horn variables  MATH (x,y) (x,y)(x,y). On it, Theorem 5.2, the
degree bound of Lemma 3.1 (valid at any fixed  MATH (x,y) (x,y)(x,y)) and
interpolation at  MATH A=(\ell+\frac{1}{2})^{2} A=(ℓ+12)2A=(\ell+\frac{1}{2})^{2} determine the diagonal amplitude
for all  MATH \alpha α\alpha. The derivatives normal to the curve are not
determined in this way: for  MATH \sigma\neq 1 σ≠1\sigma\neq 1 the coefficient relations take
the form  MATH \sigma G_{m+1}+(m-c)G_{m} σ​Gm+1+(m−c)​Gm\sigma G_{m+1}+(m-c)G_{m}, and their translation to the basis
 MATH e_{m} eme_{m} involves, in addition, the operation
 MATH \sum_{m}G_{m}e_{m}\mapsto\sum_{m}mG_{m}e_{m} ∑mGm​em↦∑mm​Gm​em\sum_{m}G_{m}e_{m}\mapsto\sum_{m}mG_{m}e_{m}, which is not expressible through
rational operations and shifts in  MATH Z ZZ. This operation is studied in
Section 6.






6. The index operator and the derivatives normal to the curve




By Remark 5.5, extending the curve contiguity away from
 MATH \sigma=1 σ=1\sigma=1 requires the multiplication of the coefficient  MATH G_{m} GmG_{m} by its
index  MATH m mm. We show that this operation is a first-order differential
operator in the normal variable, and deduce a recurrence which
determines every derivative normal to the curve of the
half-odd-integer diagonal amplitudes.




For arbitrary  MATH p,q p,qp,q with  MATH pq\neq 0 p​q≠0pq\neq 0 and  MATH p+q\neq 0 p+q≠0p+q\neq 0, put
 MATH \sigma=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q} σ=1p+1q\sigma=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q} and  MATH \rho=\frac{p}{q}+\frac{q}{p} ρ=pq+qp\rho=\frac{p}{q}+\frac{q}{p}, and let




 MATH F_{\ell}(Z;\sigma,\rho)=\sum_{m\geq 0}G_{m}^{(\ell)}e_{m}(Z),\qquad e_{m}(Z)=\frac{1}{(Z-1)\cdots(Z-m)}. Fℓ​(Z,σ,ρ)=∑m≥0Gm(ℓ)​em​(Z),em​(Z)=1(Z−1)⋯(Z−m).F_{\ell}(Z;\sigma,\rho)=\sum_{m\geq 0}G_{m}^{(\ell)}e_{m}(Z),\qquad e_{m}(Z)=\frac{1}{(Z-1)\cdots(Z-m)}.



The two invariants recover the symmetric functions of  MATH p,q p,qp,q:

 MATH pq=\frac{\rho+2}{\sigma^{2}},\qquad p+q=\frac{\rho+2}{\sigma}. p​q=ρ+2σ2,p+q=ρ+2σ.pq=\frac{\rho+2}{\sigma^{2}},\qquad p+q=\frac{\rho+2}{\sigma}.

 MATH (Z,\sigma,\rho) (Z,σ,ρ)(Z,\sigma,\rho) for every integer  MATH \ell\geq 0 ℓ≥0\ell\geq 0.

Branch convention.




All multivalued functions in this and
the following subsections are continued from a common germ at the
origin of the path described below: the Appell/Kampé de Fériet series at
 MATH (x,y) (x,y)(x,y) small, the reduced Gauss function through the argument path
 MATH 4x(\lambda)(1-x(\lambda))=2\lambda+2i\lambda(1-\lambda) 4​x​(λ)​(1−x⁡(λ))=2​λ+2​i​λ​(1−λ)4x(\lambda)(1-x(\lambda))=2\lambda+2i\lambda(1-\lambda), which stays in the upper half-plane
and reaches  MATH 2+i0 2+i​02+i0; every boundary value written
 MATH {}_{2}F_{1}^{\uparrow}(\cdots;2) F↑12​(⋯,2){}_{2}F_{1}^{\uparrow}(\cdots;2),  MATH \log(1-z)\to-i\pi log⁡(1−z)→−i​π\log(1-z)\to-i\pi,  MATH 2^{+} 2+2^{+} or  MATH -1-i0 −1−i​0-1-i0
is the one obtained along this path, and the lower determinations
(denoted  MATH \downarrow ↓\downarrow) are their Schwarz reflections. Replacing the
upper boundary value by the lower one changes the functions; no identity
below mixes the two determinations except where both appear explicitly,
as in Appendix A.4.




The Kampé de Fériet notation in Lemma 7.2 hides an additional
one-variable structure. We make it explicit because it removes one of the
two Horn variables before any asymptotic or  MATH 2 22-adic argument is made.

 MATH \mathcal{F}(\alpha,\beta;Z;x,y):=F_{3}\!\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{2}-\alpha,\frac{1}{2}-\beta;\frac{1}{2}+\alpha,\frac{1}{2}+\beta\\[2.84526pt]

Proof.

germ near  MATH x=0 x=0x=0. Analytic continuation of this common germ along any path

parameters); no passage to the large- MATH Z ZZ limit is used in establishing it.

Proof.

 MATH \alpha α\alpha. Since  MATH A=\alpha^{2} A=α2A=\alpha^{2} and the left-hand side is even in  MATH \alpha α\alpha,
 MATH \partial_{A}|_{A=0}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}^{2}|_{\alpha=0} ∂A|A=0=12∂α2|α=0\partial_{A}|_{A=0}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}^{2}|_{\alpha=0}. The function
 MATH \mathcal{H} ℋ\mathcal{H} is itself even in  MATH \alpha α\alpha, so  MATH \partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{H}(0;Z)=0 ∂αℋ⁡(0,Z)=0\partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{H}(0;Z)=0;
therefore the second logarithmic derivative is
 MATH \partial_{\alpha}^{2}\mathcal{H}(0;Z)/\mathcal{H}(0;Z) ∂α2ℋ⁡(0,Z)/ℋ⁡(0,Z)\partial_{\alpha}^{2}\mathcal{H}(0;Z)/\mathcal{H}(0;Z). Finally,  MATH F=1+O(Z^{-1}) F=1+O⁡(Z−1)F=1+O(Z^{-1}) coefficientwise, so its formal logarithm at
 MATH Z=\infty Z=∞Z=\infty is uniquely defined. Since (38) is an exact
identity for generic fixed  MATH Z ZZ, the formal inverse-power expansion used in
this paper may be taken on either side; the coefficients inherited from the
left-hand inverse-factorial series are therefore exactly those in
(39). No assertion about convergence of the resulting
 MATH Z^{-1} Z−1Z^{-1} series is needed.
∎

7.2. The boundary split and the transverse quadrature




By Corollary 7.4, the series  MATH \sum_{n}g_{n}Z^{-n} ∑ngn​Z−n\sum_{n}g_{n}Z^{-n} is

quadratures. Throughout,  MATH s=(1-Z)/2 s=(1−Z)/2s=(1-Z)/2,
 MATH a=s-\frac{\alpha}{2} a=s−α2a=s-\frac{\alpha}{2},  MATH b=s+\frac{\alpha}{2} b=s+α2b=s+\frac{\alpha}{2}, and  MATH Z>1 Z>1Z>1 is real, so  MATH s<0 s<0s<0; the

7.3. Formal quadratures and the isolation of the oscillation

Remark 7.23.



The transfer (71) is invertible away from  MATH s^{2}=\eta^{2} s2=η2s^{2}=\eta^{2}, and
it is two-dimensional: formula (74) exchanges the
derivative  MATH J_{s} JsJ_{s} for the adjacent value  MATH R_{s+1} Rs+1R_{s+1} but does not eliminate
it. The recurrence therefore determines  MATH (R_{s},J_{s}) (Rs,Js)(R_{s},J_{s}) on a class
 MATH s_{*}+\mathbb{Z} s∗+ℤs_{*}+\mathbb{Z} once the pair  MATH (R_{s_{*}},J_{s_{*}}) (Rs∗,Js∗)(R_{s_{*}},J_{s_{*}}) is known at one point
of the class. Terminating points  MATH s=-N s=−Ns=-N cannot be used for this
purpose, because  MATH 2s=-2N 2​s=−2​N2s=-2N is then a pole of the denominator parameter
away from  MATH \eta=0 η=0\eta=0. The next subsection supplies these initial values,
which we call anchors.

7.6. The asymptotic matching: proof of Theorem 1.6




This subsection proves Theorem 1.6. The real part is
obtained first, directly from Corollary 1.4. The imaginary
part is obtained by an asymptotic matching in the lower half-plane; the
estimates on which it relies are stated in Lemma 7.27 and
proved in Appendix A.

 MATH \operatorname{Re}\mathcal{A}(s)=\frac{1}{16}\,\psi^{\prime}\!\Bigl(\frac{1-s}{2}\Bigr)-\frac{1}{16}\,\psi^{\prime}\!\Bigl(1-\frac{s}{2}\Bigr)+\frac{1}{4}\,\psi^{\prime}(1-s)-\frac{\pi^{2}}{8}\,\sec^{2}\frac{\pi s}{2}, Re⁡𝒜⁡(s)=116​ψ′​(1−s2)−116​ψ′​(1−s2)+14​ψ′​(1−s)−π28​sec2⁡π​s2,\operatorname{Re}\mathcal{A}(s)=\frac{1}{16}\,\psi^{\prime}\!\Bigl(\frac{1-s}{2}\Bigr)-\frac{1}{16}\,\psi^{\prime}\!\Bigl(1-\frac{s}{2}\Bigr)+\frac{1}{4}\,\psi^{\prime}(1-s)-\frac{\pi^{2}}{8}\,\sec^{2}\frac{\pi s}{2},

Proof.

Corollary 9.2.



In the class of pairs




 MATH (\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q})\in Z^{-1}\,\mathbb{C}[[x,y]][[Z^{-1}]]^{2} (𝗉,𝗊)∈Z−1​ℂ​[[x,y]]​[[Z−1]]2(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q})\in Z^{-1}\,\mathbb{C}[[x,y]][[Z^{-1}]]^{2}



satisfying the compatibility  MATH \Theta_{y}\mathsf{p}=\Theta_{x}\mathsf{q} Θy​𝗉=Θx​𝗊\Theta_{y}\mathsf{p}=\Theta_{x}\mathsf{q},
system (95)–(96) has at most one solution for the
fixed diagonal background  MATH \mathsf{u},\mathsf{v},\mathsf{t} 𝗎,𝗏,𝗍\mathsf{u},\mathsf{v},\mathsf{t}; hence the solution obtained by differentiating
 MATH \mathcal{V}_{1} 𝒱1\mathcal{V}_{1} is the unique formal solution with zero constant term in  MATH Z^{-1} Z−1Z^{-1}.




Proof.



Existence is already supplied by  MATH \mathsf{p}=\Theta_{x}\mathcal{V}_{1} 𝗉=Θx​𝒱1\mathsf{p}=\Theta_{x}\mathcal{V}_{1} and
 MATH \mathsf{q}=\Theta_{y}\mathcal{V}_{1} 𝗊=Θy​𝒱1\mathsf{q}=\Theta_{y}\mathcal{V}_{1}. For uniqueness, let two such solutions be
given and denote their difference by  MATH (\mathsf{p}^{\circ},\mathsf{q}^{\circ}) (𝗉∘,𝗊∘)(\mathsf{p}^{\circ},\mathsf{q}^{\circ}). The forcing
terms cancel. Because  MATH F=1+O(Z^{-1}) F=1+O⁡(Z−1)F=1+O(Z^{-1}), one has  MATH \mathsf{u},\mathsf{v},\mathsf{t}=O(Z^{-1}) 𝗎,𝗏,𝗍=O⁡(Z−1)\mathsf{u},\mathsf{v},\mathsf{t}=O(Z^{-1}). Suppose,
for contradiction, that the difference is nonzero and let  MATH n\geq 1 n≥1n\geq 1 be the
least index for which the coefficient of  MATH Z^{-n} Z−nZ^{-n} in either  MATH \mathsf{p}^{\circ} 𝗉∘\mathsf{p}^{\circ} or
 MATH \mathsf{q}^{\circ} 𝗊∘\mathsf{q}^{\circ} is nonzero. Multiplication by  MATH Z ZZ makes the left-hand
side of the corresponding difference equation have a nonzero term of order
 MATH Z^{-(n-1)} Z−(n−1)Z^{-(n-1)}. Every term on its right-hand side has order at least
 MATH Z^{-n} Z−nZ^{-n}: the Euler operators do not change the order in  MATH Z^{-1} Z−1Z^{-1}, multiplication
by  MATH x xx or  MATH y yy does not change it, and multiplication by  MATH \mathsf{u},\mathsf{v},\mathsf{t} 𝗎,𝗏,𝗍\mathsf{u},\mathsf{v},\mathsf{t} raises it by
at least one. Thus the coefficient of  MATH Z^{-(n-1)} Z−(n−1)Z^{-(n-1)} on the right is zero, a
contradiction. Hence  MATH \mathsf{p}^{\circ}=\mathsf{q}^{\circ}=0 𝗉∘=𝗊∘=0\mathsf{p}^{\circ}=\mathsf{q}^{\circ}=0.
∎





The system (93)–(94) is nonlinear; its first
transverse variation is linear, with coefficients given by the diagonal
fields  MATH \mathsf{u},\mathsf{v},\mathsf{t} 𝗎,𝗏,𝗍\mathsf{u},\mathsf{v},\mathsf{t}.

Remark 10.6.



The individual terms  MATH c_{r,j} cr,jc_{r,j} have small valuations (at most of the
order of  MATH \log_{2}r log2⁡r\log_{2}r), whereas their sum is divisible by exactly
 MATH 2^{4(r-1)} 24​(r−1)2^{4(r-1)}. The valuation of  MATH \operatorname{Im}g_{2r-1} Im⁡g2​r−1\operatorname{Im}g_{2r-1} therefore
cannot be read off from the individual terms of the convolution
 MATH \operatorname{Im}\operatorname{Num}\cdot\operatorname{Den}^{-1} Im⁡Num⋅Den−1\operatorname{Im}\operatorname{Num}\cdot\operatorname{Den}^{-1}.






11. Application: Jacobi zeros for  MATH \alpha,\beta>-1 α,β>−1\alpha,\beta>-1




Throughout this section  MATH \alpha,\beta>-1 α,β>−1\alpha,\beta>-1, so that  MATH P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)} Pn(α,β)P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}
has  MATH n nn real simple zeros in  MATH (-1,1) (−1,1)(-1,1), ordered as in the Introduction.
The purpose of the section is to illustrate that the phase expansion
(3) can be evaluated in exact arithmetic, with an
accuracy away from the endpoints limited only by the truncation order.
It is not an optimised algorithm, and no timing comparison with the
specialised Gauss–Jacobi solvers
[11, 14, 13] is intended.
For related asymptotic analyses of Jacobi polynomials and their zeros,
see [7, 8].




By (5) the phase constants vanish for  MATH \alpha=\beta α=β\alpha=\beta, so
the Legendre and Gegenbauer cases need none; for  MATH \alpha\neq\beta α≠β\alpha\neq\beta they
are needed. Their denominators are known: by [17],
 MATH 2^{E_{r}}\kappa_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\mu_{\alpha},\mu_{\beta}] 2Er​κr∈ℤ2​[μα,μβ]2^{E_{r}}\kappa_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\mu_{\alpha},\mu_{\beta}] with  MATH E_{r}=3r-1+\nu_{2}((r-1)!) Er=3​r−1+ν2​((r−1)!)E_{r}=3r-1+\nu_{2}((r-1)!), and the
odd part of  MATH \den\kappa_{r} den⁡κr\den\kappa_{r} divides
 MATH \operatorname{lcm}(1,3,\dots,2r-1) lcm⁡(1,3,…,2​r−1)\operatorname{lcm}(1,3,\dots,2r-1). The denominators of
 MATH \kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{4} κ1,…,κ4\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{4} as polynomials in  MATH (A,B) (A,B)(A,B) are
 MATH 4=2^{2} 4=224=2^{2},  MATH 96=2^{5}\!\cdot\!3 96=25⋅396=2^{5}\!\cdot\!3,  MATH 7680=2^{9}\!\cdot\!15 7680=29⋅157680=2^{9}\!\cdot\!15 and
 MATH 86016=2^{12}\!\cdot\!21 86016=212⋅2186016=2^{12}\!\cdot\!21.




Solving (3) by Newton with a per-node least-term gate (the
truncation order is the last index before the correction terms stop
decreasing), then polishing by a safeguarded Newton on  MATH P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)} Pn(α,β)P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}
confined to the cell bounded by the midpoints of the neighbouring reference
angles, gives the following maximum errors in  MATH \theta θ\theta. The reference
zeros are independent of the predictor: they are obtained from
 MATH P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)} Pn(α,β)P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)} itself in  MATH 40 4040-digit multiprecision arithmetic,
and each tabulated error is measured against that reference. In the
table, “bare” is the leading approximation (1) (no
correction terms) over all nodes  MATH k=1,\dots,n k=1,…,nk=1,\dots,n; “gated” is the
gated Newton solution of  MATH \Xi(\theta)=(k-\frac{1}{2})\pi Ξ⁡(θ)=(k−12)​π\Xi(\theta)=(k-\frac{1}{2})\pi, over all nodes
or over the bulk; “polished” is the result after the final Newton
step on  MATH P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)} Pn(α,β)P_{n}^{(\alpha,\beta)}.

 MATH k=1 k=1k=1 and  MATH k=n k=nk=n is the known endpoint effect, where an expansion of

Computational checks




The identities of this paper have been checked independently by exact
computation in finite ranges, in rational, Gaussian-rational or symbolic
arithmetic. In particular, the numbers  MATH g_{n} gng_{n} were computed for
 MATH n\leq 41 n≤41n\leq 41 directly from the amplitude (6), and they agree with
(13) (real and imaginary parts) and with
(98); the relation (88) was checked
against these values for  MATH m\leq 19 m≤19m\leq 19; the valuation formula
(87) was checked for  MATH m\leq 159 m≤159m\leq 159; and the valuation
formulas of Section 10 were checked for  MATH r\leq 21 r≤21r\leq 21.
The identities of Section 7 and
Appendix A that involve boundary values, lattice sums
and their asymptotic expansions were checked numerically to at least
 MATH 25 2525 digits. None of these computations is used in the proofs, with
the exception of the finite verification in
Proposition 7.37.





Appendix A The sectorial estimates




This appendix proves the estimates stated in Lemma 7.27,
the vanishing of the constant  MATH c_{*}(s_{0}) c∗​(s0)c_{*}(s_{0}) of the quadrature
(48) on every admissible lattice
(Theorem A.12), and Theorem 1.6. The order
is as follows: coefficient bounds and the expansion of the amplitude in
a bilateral sector (Subsection A.1); uniform
asymptotics of the closed blocks (Subsection A.2); the
uniform expansion of the forcing  MATH \widehat{\mathsf{f}}_{\Gamma} 𝖿^Γ\widehat{\mathsf{f}}_{\Gamma} and of its
lattice sum  MATH T TT (Subsection A.3); the relation
between the two boundary determinations, from which the vertical decay
of the quotient  MATH Q QQ follows (Subsection A.4); a
classification lemma for periodic meromorphic functions and the
vanishing of  MATH c_{*}(s_{0}) c∗​(s0)c_{*}(s_{0}) (Subsection A.5); and the proof
of Theorem 1.6 (Subsection A.6). The
only classical inputs are Stirling’s formula in sectors, the
Euler–Maclaurin (Hurwitz) expansion, and the removability of bounded
isolated singularities.

Proposition A.3.

Proof.

Remark A.4.



The cone  MATH |\operatorname{Re}Z|\leq\theta_{0}|\operatorname{Im}Z| |Re⁡Z|≤θ0​|Im⁡Z||\operatorname{Re}Z|\leq\theta_{0}|\operatorname{Im}Z| with
 MATH \theta_{0}<1 θ0<1\theta_{0}<1 cannot be widened by the termwise estimate above: for
directions closer to the real axis the individual terms  MATH G_{n}e_{n} Gn​enG_{n}e_{n} with
 MATH n\asymp|Z| n≍|Z|n\asymp|Z| grow exponentially and the series converges only through
cancellation. The applications of Lemma 7.27 in this paper
do not require a wider aperture: the matching of
Subsection A.6 is performed along a single vertical
line, the decay statements of Subsection A.5 concern
vertical strips, and the coefficients of a Poincaré expansion along
one ray are unique. The mirror symmetry used repeatedly below is the
Schwarz reflection  MATH \mathcal{A}^{\downarrow}(s)=\overline{\mathcal{A}^{\uparrow}(\bar{s})} 𝒜↓​(s)=𝒜↑​(s¯)¯\mathcal{A}^{\downarrow}(s)=\overline{\mathcal{A}^{\uparrow}(\bar{s})}, valid because all four
blocks  MATH \mathsf{S}_{0},\mathsf{K}_{0},\mathsf{S}_{2},\mathsf{K}_{2} 𝖲0,𝖪0,𝖲2,𝖪2\mathsf{S}_{0},\mathsf{K}_{0},\mathsf{S}_{2},\mathsf{K}_{2} are real on
the real axis.

(103)

whose right-hand Gamma arguments have real part tending to  MATH +\infty +∞+\infty.

 MATH \Bigl|\frac{\Gamma(x)}{\Gamma(x+\frac{1}{2})}\Bigr|=\frac{|\mathrm{B}(x,\tfrac{1}{2})|}{\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{2})}\leq\frac{\mathrm{B}(\operatorname{Re}x,\tfrac{1}{2})}{\sqrt{\pi}}\leq C\,(\operatorname{Re}x)^{-1/2} |Γ⁡(x)Γ⁡(x+12)|=|B⁡(x,12)|Γ⁡(12)≤B⁡(Re⁡x,12)π≤C(Rex)−1/2\Bigl|\frac{\Gamma(x)}{\Gamma(x+\frac{1}{2})}\Bigr|=\frac{|\mathrm{B}(x,\tfrac{1}{2})|}{\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{2})}\leq\frac{\mathrm{B}(\operatorname{Re}x,\tfrac{1}{2})}{\sqrt{\pi}}\leq C\,(\operatorname{Re}x)^{-1/2}

 MATH \widehat{\mathsf{r}}(\varsigma)=-\varsigma\Bigl[\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{1-\varsigma}{2}\bigr)}{\Gamma\bigl(1-\frac{\varsigma}{2}\bigr)}\Bigr]^{2}\bigl(1+O(e^{-\pi\operatorname{Im}\varsigma})\bigr), 𝗋^​(ς)=−ς​[Γ⁡(1−ς2)Γ⁡(1−ς2)]2​(1+O⁡(e−π​Im⁡ς)),\widehat{\mathsf{r}}(\varsigma)=-\varsigma\Bigl[\frac{\Gamma\bigl(\frac{1-\varsigma}{2}\bigr)}{\Gamma\bigl(1-\frac{\varsigma}{2}\bigr)}\Bigr]^{2}\bigl(1+O(e^{-\pi\operatorname{Im}\varsigma})\bigr),

∎




Lemma A.7.



 MATH \widehat{\mathsf{f}}_{\Gamma} 𝖿^Γ\widehat{\mathsf{f}}_{\Gamma} is meromorphic on  MATH \mathbb{C} ℂ\mathbb{C} with:

Proposition A.8.

 MATH \Bigl|T(s)-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\omega_{j}\,s^{-j}\Bigr|\ \leq\ C_{\delta,N}\,|s|^{-N-1}\ +\ C_{\delta}\,\frac{e^{-\pi\operatorname{Im}s}}{\operatorname{Im}s}. |T⁡(s)−∑j=1Nωj​s−j|≤Cδ,N​|s|−N−1+Cδ​e−π​Im⁡sIm⁡s.\Bigl|T(s)-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\omega_{j}\,s^{-j}\Bigr|\ \leq\ C_{\delta,N}\,|s|^{-N-1}\ +\ C_{\delta}\,\frac{e^{-\pi\operatorname{Im}s}}{\operatorname{Im}s}.

Theorem A.12.

 MATH T(s+2)-T(s)=\widehat{\mathsf{f}}_{\Gamma}(s+1) T⁡(s+2)−T⁡(s)=𝖿^Γ​(s+1)T(s+2)-T(s)=\widehat{\mathsf{f}}_{\Gamma}(s+1); and
 MATH \tan\frac{\pi(s+1)}{2}=-\cot\frac{\pi s}{2} tan⁡π⁡(s+1)2=−cot⁡π​s2\tan\frac{\pi(s+1)}{2}=-\cot\frac{\pi s}{2}, so

(Lemma 7.15). Hence

A.6. Proof of Theorem 1.6

 MATH S_{\psi}(s)=s^{-1}+\tfrac{1}{2}s^{-2}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{B}_{2k}s^{-2k-1} Sψ​(s)=s−1+12​s−2+∑k≥1ℬ2​k​s−2​k−1S_{\psi}(s)=s^{-1}+\tfrac{1}{2}s^{-2}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{B}_{2k}s^{-2k-1} denote

Lemma A.14.
